The deleted files from the pre-Epic-2 architecture (`src/tmux.ts`, `src/peer-pid.ts`, `src/sessions.ts`, `hooks/permission-relay.sh`, `hooks/ask-relay.sh`) are absent: agent-director owns the tmux integration and Claude session-id state, the SR-2.1 poller replaces the hook-based long-poll relay, and AskUserQuestion is denied at the template level (SR-3.1). The `src/trail-cli.ts` and `src/trail-query.ts` CLI query wrappers (removed in `b.8tm`) are also absent — the trail file itself is the surface; see [Trail file location and query recipes](#trail-file-location-and-query-recipes--sr-v-5--sr-v-6).

## Data Flow

### Inbound (Slack → Claude Code)

Inbound delivery lives in `src/persona-routing.ts`. Each persona has its own Socket Mode connection (`src/persona-connections.ts`), and each connection's events reach the event router (`src/persona-event-router.ts`) tagged with that persona's key; `server.ts` only wires the router to the routing instance. The steps below run in code order, once per receiving persona P.

1. Slack message arrives on P's own connection (`message` or `app_mention` event). The event router logs `[slack] RAW <event> event persona=<P's key>: <first 300 chars of the event JSON>` and calls `receive(event, ack, <P's key>)`: P is the only receiver. Two personas in one channel each receive the event on their own connection, and each runs its own pipeline.
2. **Intake.** `receive` acks the event first; an ack failure is logged and handling continues. Then, for the receiving persona P (the intake also accepts a key list, each run concurrently), the archive is written and P's pipeline runs. The archive write is unconditional and comes before dedupe and any decision; it is keyed by (channel, ts), so a message received by several personas, or received twice by one, is stored once (see `src/message-archive.ts`). Each persona's run is isolated: a throw is logged as `[slack] persona-routing: error handling event for persona <ref>` with a `describeSlackCallFailure` tail and never stops another persona's run.
3. **Dedupe** (b.av2 SR-4.1). P's own dedupe store (`src/inbound-dedupe.ts`) records the event's key: (channel, ts), or (channel, ts, edited.ts) for an event that carries a top-level `edited.ts`. A key P first saw less than 10 minutes ago (`INBOUND_DEDUPE_RETENTION_MS`) is a duplicate and stops here: no decision, dispatch, reaction, recovery, post or log line. This collapses the `message` / `app_mention` pair Slack sends for a channel @mention and any Slack redelivery (for example after a reconnect). An edit keeps the message's ts, so `edited.ts` tells an edited event apart from the original: an edit that adds P's mention arrives as an `app_mention` with the original ts and an `edited` object, gets its own key and reaches P once, with `via` `mention`, while a redelivery of that same edited event is a duplicate. A `message_changed` event is keyed like any other event but is then dropped by the decision (step 4.1, not a deliverable subtype), so an edit that adds `<!here>` / `<!channel>` wakes nobody. The window runs from the first sighting; a duplicate does not extend it. Expired keys are pruned lazily on each new record, with no timer. An event missing its channel or ts is not keyed and carries on as new. The routing instance keeps one store per persona key, created on first use, so it survives a reopen of P's connection; the stores are in memory only and are empty after a server restart.
4. **Decision** (`decideDelivery` in `src/delivery-decision.ts`, pure; see its Module Map entry). A key that is no applied persona first logs `[slack] persona-routing: no applied persona with key=<key> — event not delivered to it`. The decision runs against P in b.av2 SR-4.2 order:
   1. A subtype other than `file_share`, `bot_message`, `thread_broadcast` or `me_message`, or no channel, is dropped. An event with neither `user` nor `bot_id` is dropped as `no-author`.
   2. P's own messages (author `user` = P's bot user ID, or the event's `bot_id` = P's bot ID) are dropped. This self-exclusion is the only author filter: posts by people, bots, integrations, webhooks and other personas are delivered by the rules below, and nothing counts, throttles or arbitrates exchanges between personas.
   3. Conversation kind (b.av2 SR-4.3). A DM is an event with `channel_type` `im`; as a defensive fallback, an event without `channel_type` whose conversation ID starts with `D` is treated as a DM too (Slack does not send `app_mention` for 1:1 DMs, so a DM arrives as a `message` event with `channel_type` `im`). A DM that passed steps 1 and 2 depends only on P's `dm.enabled`: on, it is delivered with `via` `dm`, whatever the text mentions; off, it is dropped as `dm-disabled` and logs one `persona-dm-dropped` line naming P, the conversation, the ts and `dm.enabled`, with no Slack call. A DM is decided only against the persona whose connection received it, and nothing in this path is channel-specific, so a persona with zero channels and DMs on receives its DMs the same way. A group DM is recognised only by `channel_type` `mpim`. The app does not subscribe to `message.mpim` (the manifest has `message.im` but no `message.mpim`), so such events are rare; one that arrives is dropped as `group-dm` with the plain drop line, whatever `dm.enabled` says, and never logs `persona-dm-dropped`. Neither a DM nor an `mpim` event reaches the channel rules below, so neither logs `unclaimed-channel`. An `app_mention` in a group DM carries no `channel_type` and a `G…` or `C…` conversation ID, so it is decided by the channel rules below: it is not delivered unless a persona lists that ID, and it normally logs `unclaimed-channel`.
   4. A channel P is not configured into is not delivered. When no applied persona lists the channel, one `unclaimed-channel` line names the channel and P; when another applied persona lists it, nothing is logged.
   5. A `delivery: all` channel delivers.
   6. A `delivery: mentions` channel delivers when the text contains P's user mention (`<@P>` or `<@P|label>`) or `<!here>` / `<!channel>` (with or without a label); `<!everyone>` and user-group mentions do not count.

   A delivered message carries `via`, how it reached P; the first match wins: `dm`, `mention`, `broadcast`, `receive_all_shared` (a `delivery: all` channel that at least one other applied persona also receives with `delivery: all`), `receive_all`. Only a DM produces `dm`, and a DM never produces any other value.

   The two classed lines are built by `formatPersonaDiagnostic`, e.g. `[slack] unclaimed-channel: personas[<i>] "<name>" (key=<key>): message in channel <C…> not delivered: no applied persona lists this channel` and `[slack] persona-dm-dropped: personas[<i>] "<name>" (key=<key>): direct message in conversation <D…> ts=<ts> dropped: dm.enabled is off for this persona` (the `ts=` part is left out when the event has no ts). The other drop reasons log `[slack] persona "<name>" (key=<key>) dropped message from channel=<C…> user=<U…>: <reason>`, with `bot_id=<B…>` in place of `user=` for an author without `user`. No drop line carries message text.

   For when a confirmed change takes effect here, see Session Lifecycle, [In-place update](#in-place-update-bav2-sr-86).
5. **Author label and session lookup.** The author's name is resolved first, for the meta of a delivered message and for the notice of a lost one: through P's client, or for an author without `user` (a webhook or `bot_message`) from the event itself (`username`, else `bot_profile.name`, else its bot ID) with no Slack call. A failed user-name lookup falls back to the user ID and logs one line (`[slack] persona-routing: user-name lookup for persona <ref> failed, using the user ID: …`); the message is not lost. Then P's session is read with `getSessionByPersona(P's key)`, after that await, so a session replaced or dropped during the lookup is seen. With no registered, connected session the message is dropped (`[slack] No live session for persona <ref> chat_id=<C…> — dropping message`) and the b.kvq no-session branch runs (step 7).
6. **Stream check.** The dispatch line `[slack] Dispatching to persona <ref> chat_id=<C…> cwd="…" mcpSessionId=… hasGetStream=… connected=… text="<first 80 chars>"` is logged. If the session has lost its `_GET_stream` (`hasGetStreamKey` on the session's transport false), `notification()` would vanish silently, so it is not sent: `[slack] DROP: no _GET_stream for persona <ref> chat_id=<C…> …` is logged and the b.9cj streamless branch runs (step 7). Nothing is awaited from the session read to the send in step 8, so the probe and the send act on the same session (b.9cj).
7. **Lost message** (b.av2 SR-4.6, SR-7.3; b.kvq / b.9cj). Both branches are handled by `handleLostMessage` in `persona-routing.ts`. The message is not delivered, gets no ack reaction and no ack-tracker entry, and is never saved or replayed. `decideLostMessageState` (`lost-message.ts`) applies the shared guards over P's up state and real restart state, keyed by P, first match wins: P not up (the routing's injected `isPersonaUp` answers false: P is `retrying` or `broken`, or unknown to the controller) → `not-up`, and no restart is started, since P's own recovery launches it once it is up (a persona that is not up forwards no Slack event, so `not-up` is reached only when P stops being up between receiving the message and the session lookup, as with a Web API call refused for its bot token, or by a message the old half of a destructive modify receives after its bring-up state is cancelled and before its connection is stopped); a restart already pending or active → `restarting`; auto-restart disabled (`session_restart_delay: 0`) → `auto-restart-disabled`; P at the failure cap → `restart-limit-reached`; otherwise `starting-now`, and only then `scheduleRestart(key, cwd, undefined, { humanTrigger: true })` is called (P's `working_directory` in the no-session branch, the session's `cwd` in the streamless branch). Either branch then raises exactly one lost-message notice (`buildLostMessageNotice`) through the injected `notify`, the per-persona notifier's `notify`: it goes to P's destination, names the sender and the recovery state, and carries no message text. It takes the notifier's path like every persona notice (see the `persona-notifier.ts` entry and Permission Relay, [Destination hold](#destination-hold-bav2-sr-71-failures)). Nothing else is posted in the source conversation; when the destination is that conversation, the notice is the one post there. The notice is awaited; a failing `notify` logs one `persona-routing: lost-message notice … failed` line and is never thrown.

   **Invariant: lost-message reports go only to the persona's destination.** A server message in the conversation a message came from would give away, in a shared channel, a persona passing as a person. So no delivery failure is ever reported in the source conversation because the message came from there, for a channel or a DM source alike; when the source conversation is also the destination, the destination's one notice is the only post there. Future delivery or queueing work (b.4vj, guaranteed inbound delivery) is bound by the same rule and must report to the destination.
8. **Delivery.** P's session's MCP Server sends `notifications/claude/channel` (awaited) to its Claude Code client, with P's own mention token (`<@P>` or `<@P|label>`) stripped from the text; other mentions and broadcasts stay. Only P's session receives it. The meta, rendered by the harness as attributes of the `<channel>` tag, is `chat_id` (the source conversation), `message_id`, `user` (the author label from step 5), the author's `user_id` (or, for an author without `user`, its `bot_id`; never both), `ts`, `via` from step 4, `thread_ts` when present, and `attachment_count` / `attachments` when the message has files.
9. **Ack reaction** (b.av2 SR-4.5). Only after the send in step 8 has succeeded does P react, so only a dispatched message gets a reaction: a drop, a dedupe duplicate, a lost message (step 7) or a send that throws gets no reaction and no ack-tracker entry. If the server-wide `ack_reaction` is set (`deps.getReplySettings()`, the start-time value), `trackAck(P's key, channelId, messageTs)` records P's entry first, so P's first reply always finds it, and then the reaction is added through P's own client without being awaited (a failure is ignored; the entry stays and the message is still delivered). With `ack_reaction` absent, or no client for P (dry run, or P not serving), both are skipped. When one message is dispatched to several personas (each on its own connection), each runs this step for itself, so the message carries one reaction per dispatching persona, each under that persona's own identity, and each persona holds its own entry.

Injected messages (`/interject` and cron, which posts through `/interject`) reach the persona's instance but do not come through this pipeline: they are not Slack messages, so they get no ack reaction. Their meta is unchanged, `user` and `ts` only, with no `via`; see [POST /interject](#post-interject).

### Outbound (Claude Code → Slack)

1. Claude Code calls MCP tools (`reply`, `react`, `edit_message`, etc.)
2. The tool handler resolves the calling persona at call time from the session's registered persona key (`SessionToolDeps.getPersona`). A session not yet matched to a persona, or whose key is no applied persona, gets a tool error.
3. `checkPersonaTarget()` checks the tool's target (`chat_id`, or `channel` for `fetch_messages`) against the persona as applied now, before the dry-run branch (b.av2 SR-5.1). There are three target kinds:
   - **Configured channel** — the `id` of one of the persona's configured channels, whatever its `delivery` mode. All five tools accept it.
   - **DM conversation** (`D…`) — allowed only while the persona's `dm.enabled` is on. All five tools accept it. The ID is passed to Slack as is; Slack refuses a conversation the persona's app is not in, and that comes back as a tool failure (step 4).
   - **User ID** (`U…`/`W…`) — allowed only for `reply` (its text and file uploads) and only while `dm.enabled` is on. `reply` calls `conversations.open` on the persona's own client, then posts the chunks, uploads the files and removes the ack reaction in the returned `D…` conversation, as the persona. The user ID itself never reaches a Slack write. The open runs on every such call; nothing is cached. The result names the opened conversation, so the session can use that `D…` ID for later edits, reactions, reads and thread replies. `conversations.open` needs the `im:write` bot scope. A failed open, or one that returns no conversation ID, is a tool error naming the persona and the user, and nothing is posted; when the failure is `missing_scope`, the error also names `im:write` and says to add it and re-install the app. `react`, `edit_message`, `fetch_messages` and `download_attachment` refuse a user ID whatever the switch, so no read, edit or reaction opens a DM.

   For when a confirmed change takes effect here, see Session Lifecycle, [In-place update](#in-place-update-bav2-sr-86).

   With `dm.enabled` off the persona has no DM target: every `D…`, `U…` or `W…` target on every tool is refused with the reason `DMs are off for this persona (dm.enabled is false).`, so no `conversations.open` is made and nothing is posted to, read from or reacted on in a DM. The "needs a conversation ID, not a user ID" reason applies only while DMs are on, to a user ID given to an act tool. Any other target (an unconfigured channel, a group DM, an empty value) is refused as not a configured channel; every refusal is a tool error naming the persona and the target, with no Slack call, dry run included.

   Nothing on this path assumes a configured channel, so a zero-channel persona with DMs on answers its DMs in place through the same check. The dry run of a `reply` to a user ID reports that it would open a DM and makes no `conversations.open` call. `reply` also runs `assertSendable()` on every file before any Slack call (so a refused file stops a user-ID reply before the open), and again on each file right before its upload so a file swapped in between is still caught; a refused file is a tool error.
4. The tool resolves the persona's own Web client at call time (`SessionToolDeps.clientFor`: the connection manager's long-lived client for that persona while it is serving; see `persona-start.ts`) and makes the Slack call with it, `reply` uploads (`filesUploadV2`) included, with no username or icon override. `download_attachment` authorises file fetches with that client's bot token, sent only to `https://files.slack.com` URLs. It refuses external (non-Slack-hosted) files and never follows a redirect off that origin with the header. A Slack call failure is logged token-safely and returned as a tool error naming the persona and, for a DM or user-ID target, that target. A failure after a user ID's DM was opened (a post or upload) also names the opened conversation: `Tool "reply" failed for persona <ref> on DM target "U…" (conversation D…): the tool call failed (<code>).`, with the same `(conversation D…)` part in the log line. A missing client or token is also a tool error.
5. `reply` splits its text with `chunkText(text, reply_chunk_limit, reply_chunk_mode)` (`lib.ts`), reading the server-wide settings through `SessionToolDeps.getReplySettings()` (start-time values; defaults 4000 and `newline`). After the first chunk posts (once per call, not per chunk), if `message_id` was provided, `SessionToolDeps.consumeAck(persona key, conversation, message_id)` finds the calling persona's own tracked entry and `ack_reaction` is set, that persona's reaction is removed via `reactions.remove` through its own client (a failure is ignored). The conversation is the one posted to (for a user-ID target, the opened `D…` conversation). Another persona's entry and reaction on the same message are left alone until that persona replies with the `message_id`; a persona that never reacted finds no entry and removes nothing.

### Slack Reply Guard (Stop hook)

`stop-hooks/slack-reply-guard.sh` runs in every bot session whose config dir the bootstrap patched: the start pass (startup step 4a-ter) or the launch-time pass before each spawn or resume. It reads stdin once, reads the transcript once with a single `jq` pass, and fails open (exit 0) on any error, missing input, or malformed transcript. Its only exit-2 path is step 8.

1. **Per-persona gate.** The first argument is the record directory `<state dir>/reply-guard`. The guard continues only when the argument is non-empty, `CSCB_PERSONA` matches `^[a-z0-9_]{1,49}$`, and the record `<dir>/<CSCB_PERSONA>` is a regular, readable file reading exactly `true` (one trailing newline tolerated). Every other case, `false` included, exits 0 silently. How the record is written and deleted: see Configuration, reply-guard/<key>; a persona teardown deletes it.
2. **jq check.** A missing `jq` logs one stderr line and exits 0. A persona stopped at step 1 never gets this warning.
3. **Retry pass-through.** `stop_hook_active=true` exits 0 immediately, so the reminder fires at most once per turn. A missing, unreadable or empty transcript also exits 0.
4. **Trigger run.** A real user entry has `type=="user"`, is not `isSidechain` or `isCompactSummary`, and has at least one text block. The run is every real user entry after the nearest non-sidechain assistant entry preceding the latest real user entry. Claude Code can write queued channel messages as consecutive user entries, so the run can hold several. Tool-result-only entries, sidechain entries, and compaction summaries neither join nor end the run.
5. **Envelope.** Only an opening `<channel …>` tag at the very start of an entry's text (after optional whitespace) is an envelope; a tag later in the text is quoted body and is ignored. The tag is read as whitespace-led `name="value"` pairs, left to right. Names are compared whole (`via` never matches inside a longer name), the first of a repeated name wins, and text inside one attribute's value is never read as another attribute. The envelope is Slack iff its `source` starts with `slack`.
6. **`via` test.** A Slack envelope that carries `via` is a delivered Slack message, even when the value is empty or unknown. One without `via` is injected (a scheduled prompt or an `/interject` message), whatever its `user` or `ts`. No delivered Slack message in the run → exit 0.
7. **Reply check.** An assistant `tool_use` named `mcp__slack-channel-router__reply` anywhere after the earliest delivered Slack entry in the run → exit 0. Because the whole run is checked, an injected message queued right after a delivered one does not hide it.
8. **Reminder.** Otherwise exit 2 with a declinable reminder on stderr. The `via` of the last delivered Slack envelope in the run picks the wording: one each for `dm`, `mention`, `broadcast`, `receive_all_shared` and `receive_all`, and the generic channel wording for any other value. When that envelope's `chat_id` matches `^[A-Z0-9]+$`, the wording adds `(conversation <chat_id>)` for `dm` or `(channel <chat_id>)` otherwise; a missing or non-matching `chat_id` is left out. The raw `via` value is never echoed. README.md's Slack Reply Guard section shows the texts.

The envelope is rendered by the Claude Code harness from the MCP notification's `meta`. The tag name `channel` and the attribute names the guard reads (`source`, `via`, `chat_id`) are a harness-owned contract. The guard depends on CSCB never putting `via` on injected meta: `/interject`, and cron through it, sends only `user` and `ts` (see [POST /interject](#post-interject)). It also relies on the harness escaping attribute values, since a value is read up to the next `"`.

A confirmed change to a persona's `stop_hook_bootstrap` or `claude_config_dir` (own or inherited) reaches the guard at the persona's next launch, which writes its record and runs the install rule; the apply itself writes nothing (see [Next-launch and server-wide settings](#next-launch-and-server-wide-settings-bav2-sr-86)).

Accepted limit: launched-with directories are kept in memory only and are not persisted. After a confirmed `claude_config_dir` change and a plain server restart, a still-running instance no longer counts toward the old directory it launched with. When every persona still counted toward that directory is disabled, the next pass that evaluates it (the restart's start pass, or a launch pass for a neighbour whose effective or launched-with directory it is) removes the managed Stop hook from under the instance; only that instance's reply reminder is lost, until its next launch. The README (Slack Reply Guard, Timing) and the debugging skill document it for operators.

### Permission Relay (SR-2)

Driven by polling against the agent-director Client + Block Kit click → `decide()`. The previous HTTP long-poll + hook script architecture has been deleted. Per-row Slack prompts: N concurrent `tool_use` blocks in one Claude Code assistant response produce N Slack messages, each independently allow/deny-able.

#### Wire surface (paired AD release)

CSCB consumes four AD verbs:

- `client.list({ state: ['check_permission'], label: ['service=cscb'] })` — returns spawn rows in the permission state.
- `client.get({ claude_instance_id })` — returns the row plus a plural `permission_requests` projection. Each element carries `request_token`, `request_id`, `tool_name`, `tool_input`, `requested_at`. The array may be empty (spawn has no open prompts). `null` / `undefined` in the slot is non-conforming.
- `client.decide({ claude_instance_id, decision, request_token })` — JSON snake `request_token`, CLI `--request-token`. Always required; absence yields `ErrInvalidFlags`. The wrapper `decideWithToken` in `agent-director-client.ts` is the single decide-wire serializer (SR-7.2).
- `client.getPermission({ request_token })` — single-row read, no state filter. Returns full PermissionRequestInfo plus `decision`, `decision_reason`, and `decided_at`. Not-found surfaces as `ErrPermissionRequestNotFound` (matched via `isErrPermissionRequestNotFound`).

#### In-memory state (SR-1)

`livePermissions: Map<string, LivePermission>` keyed on a composite `(claude_instance_id, request_token)` encoded by `makeCompositeKey` (null-byte separator — neither component can contain `\x00`). One entry per outstanding Slack prompt. `LivePermission` carries `claudeInstanceId`, `requestToken`, `personaKey` (the persona whose client posted the prompt; closing updates go through it), `channelId` (the conversation the prompt was posted in: the channel, or the `D…` DM conversation), `messageTs`, `requestId` (log-only — never used for routing, keying, action-id encoding, or decide-wire payload per SR-1.4), and a `handled` flag. Lifecycle helpers `getLivePermission(cid, token)`, `markHandled(cid, token)`, and `dropPermission(cid, token)` all operate on the composite key. The tick is the sole owner of `dropPermission`; the click handler may only call `markHandled` (SR-1.2).

The recorded `personaKey` and `channelId` are the only routing a posted prompt keeps. The closing update (step 5) and the click updates ([Click handler](#click-handler-sr-4)) go to that conversation through that persona's client and never resolve the persona's destination again or read its DMs switch. So a prompt stays where it was posted and stays answerable after the persona's destination changes, and a prompt posted in a DM is still updated after `dm.enabled` is turned off (b.av2 SR-5.1 exception: an update is not a post). The DMs-off scope that does not apply here is the MCP tools' outbound scoping (`checkPersonaTarget`, b.av2 SR-5.1; see Outbound), which govern posts, reads and DM opens made by a session, not updates to prompts the server already posted.

**Teardown.** A persona teardown drops the persona's tracked prompts, their not-posted records and its wedge state (`forgetPersonaPrompts`) with no closing update, so their Slack messages stay as posted. A later click on one has no effect: a click arrives only on the Slack connection of the persona that posted the prompt (b.av2 SR-7.1), and the teardown closed it.

#### Per-tick flow (SR-2)

1. `list()` → spawns currently in `check_permission`.
2. For each spawn, resolve the persona from the row's `persona` label through the applied persona set (`PollerDeps.getPersona`, read every tick; b.av2 SR-7.1). The `channel` label is never read. A row with no `persona` label logs `[slack] permission-poller: spawn <id> has no persona label — skipping`, and one naming no applied persona logs `[slack] permission-poller: spawn <id> names no applied persona (persona=<key>) — skipping`; either is skipped this tick with no `get`. The no-label line is logged once per instance ID while the row stays listed (`noLabelLogged`, b.1ix), since the start sweep keeps a pre-persona row and one can sit in `check_permission` indefinitely; an ID that a successful `list()` no longer holds is forgotten, so the row logs again if it comes back, and a failed `list()` forgets nothing. A spawn naming a persona that is no longer applied is treated like a non-conforming spawn (step 3): its live entries are exempt from the closing sweep and its wedge state is kept, since its requests may still be open in agent-director, until the persona's teardown (apply step 2) drops them. A spawn with no `persona` label gets no such exemption. A spawn whose persona is applied but not up (`PollerDeps.isPersonaUp`, the server's shared up predicate; b.av2 SR-6.4) is skipped with no `get`: nothing is posted under any identity, its instance keeps running, and its wedge detector gets no tick. Its live entries are exempt from the closing sweep and its wedge state is kept, whether or not its spawn is listed this tick, because its requests are still open in agent-director. On the first tick after the persona is up its rows are read again: a prompt opened while it was down posts then, and a tracked prompt that closed meanwhile gets its verdict through step 5. The skip emits no trail event and no `cscb.poller.row_decision`. It logs once when it starts, `[slack] permission-poller: persona "<name>" (key=<key>) is not up — skipping its rows and holding its tracked prompts until it is up`, and once when it ends, `[slack] permission-poller: persona "<name>" (key=<key>) is up again — polling its rows again` (or `[slack] permission-poller: persona=<key> is no longer applied — no longer skipping its rows` when the persona left the applied set). Then `get()` returns the plural `permission_requests` projection, inside `withOutageDetection` keyed by the persona key.
3. Non-conforming response (`permission_requests` is `null` or `undefined`): log + skip processing for that spawn this tick. The spawn's existing live entries are excluded from the closure sweep below (no state mutation — SR-2.1). A spawn naming a persona that is no longer applied (step 2) gets the same exemption.
4. For each row in `permission_requests`, compute the composite key. If not in `livePermissions`, route it to the persona's destination through the shared destination resolver (`src/persona-destination.ts`, the same resolver and DM cache the notifier uses):
   - **Re-resolve after awaits (AC 58):** a confirmed reload's step 1 can swap the applied set during any await of the tick. The poller re-reads the persona (`getPersona(key)`) after the agent-director `get`, before the wedge attempt of an empty projection and before each prompt post, so each goes to the destination applied now. A persona gone by then is handled as a row naming no applied persona (step 2): the same line, its live entries exempt from the closing sweep and its wedge state kept, and its remaining prompts in that row are not posted. The not-up check uses the read at the start of the row.
   - **Destination:** a channel in `permission_prompts` is that channel. `dm` is the persona's DM conversation with `dm.contact`, obtained with `conversations.open` on the persona's client. Slack returns the existing conversation, or opens one when none exists, so a DM-only persona's first prompt is delivered with no earlier DM. The resolved conversation is cached per persona and contact: later prompts reuse it with no second open, concurrent posts share one in-flight open, and a changed contact opens afresh (see the Module Map entry for what drops the cache).
   - **Post:** ask the destination hold for an attempt (`begin(key)`); while the persona is held, stop here (see **Held persona** below). Otherwise emit the `post_attempted` row decision, then post one `chat.postMessage` per row (no coalescing — SR-2.2) through the persona's own client (`PollerDeps.clientFor(key)`, the connection manager's long-lived client for that persona) to the destination conversation — the channel, or the returned `D…` ID, never the contact's user ID — and insert one map entry recording that conversation. For a `dm` destination the `conversations.open`, when one is needed, comes before the post on the same client. A failed open or post emits one `ok:false` `cscb.chat_post.attempted` and inserts no map entry, so the row is still untracked on the next tick; the attempt reports its outcome to the hold. A destination failure (every failed open, and every failed post except a payload error) is logged only by the hold, as its one `persona-destination-failed` line per episode, and holds the persona. A post that fails for the message itself (`MESSAGE_PAYLOAD_ERRORS`, such as `invalid_blocks` or `msg_too_long`) holds nothing and logs `[slack] permission-poller: chat.postMessage failed for <id><tail>` on every attempt; the next tick tries it again.
   - **Held persona** (an episode is open and its retry is not due yet, or a retry for it is already in flight; see [Destination hold](#destination-hold-bav2-sr-71-failures)): no Slack call, no `post_attempted`, no trail event, no line and no map entry. The prompt stays untracked and is derived again on each tick; the first tick at or after the retry's due time makes the retry, one prompt at a time per persona. The poller sets no timer of its own for this: retries of prompts ride on the tick. A request that stops being pending while its persona is held (decided in the terminal, timed out, or its spawn gone) is simply no longer derived, so it is never posted. This check comes after the refused-destination and client checks below.
   - **Refused `dm` destination** (`dm.enabled` not true or no `dm.contact`; the loader rejects both, so this is a defensive guard, checked first with `dmDestinationRefusal`): no Slack call, no `post_attempted`, no map entry. Logged once per open request: `[slack] permission-poller: "<name>" (key=<key>) has permission_prompts set to "dm" but <dm.enabled is not true|dm.contact is not set> — prompt for <id> (request_token=<token>) not posted and no DM opened`.
   - **Client unavailable** (`clientFor` returns undefined; a persona that is not up is already skipped in step 2, so this is a guard for a persona whose connection stops serving between the two checks): no Slack call, no `post_attempted`, no map entry, so a later tick retries. Logged once per open request: `[slack] permission-poller: no Slack client for "<name>" (key=<key>) — prompt for <id> (request_token=<token>) not posted; retrying on a later tick`.

   The once-per-request record of an unposted prompt is forgotten when the prompt posts or the request is no longer observed (a spawn skipped this tick keeps its records); a changed reason logs again. If the composite key is already in `livePermissions`: no-op (duplicate-tick safe). An empty projection produces no posting activity but does not exclude the spawn's live entries from closure reconciliation. An empty projection also feeds the wedge detector (below).
5. **Set-diff closure reconciliation (SR-2.4):** `tokens_in_livePermissions − tokens_seen_this_tick = newly_closed_tokens` (with non-conforming spawns' entries protected per step 3, and a not-up persona's entries held per step 2). For each newly-closed token, call `getPermission(request_token)` inside `withOutageDetection` keyed by `entry.personaKey`. On success, render the verdict (below) via one `chat.update` against `entry.channelId` + `entry.messageTs` through the client of the persona that posted it (`clientFor(entry.personaKey)`), then drop the entry. When that client is unavailable, no update is made, `[slack] permission-poller: no Slack client for "<name>" (key=<key>) — closure update for <id> token=<token> (verdict=<tag>) skipped` is logged (`persona=<key>` in place of the name and key when the persona is no longer applied), and the entry is still dropped (agent-director has already closed the request). On `ErrPermissionRequestNotFound`: render generic deny, drop, do not retry. On transient error: leave the entry alive — the next tick retries.

#### Verdict rendering (SR-5)

The closure `chat.update` produces four visually-distinct surfaces driven by `decision` + `decision_reason`:

| `decision` | `decision_reason` | Rendering |
|---|---|---|
| `allow` | `null` | `*Permission* — Allowed` |
| `deny` | `'operator'` | `*Permission* — Denied by operator` |
| `deny` | `'timeout'` | `⏱ *Permission* — Timed out` |
| `deny` | `'find_missing'` | `🪦 *Permission* — Session ended` |
| anything else | anything else | `*Permission* — Denied (closed)` (fail-closed, log) |

`ErrPermissionRequestNotFound` collapses to the same generic-deny rendering (fail-closed log + drop, no retry). `decision_reason` is read verbatim from AD; CSCB does not derive, override, or infer it (SR-5.1). Unknown enum values do not crash the poller (SR-5.2). The `chat.update` targets only the closed row's `messageTs`; sibling rows are never touched in response to one row's closure (SR-5.3).

#### Click handler (SR-4)

`handlePermissionClick` decodes `(decision, claude_instance_id, request_token)` from the action_id and calls `decideWithToken(client, { claude_instance_id, decision, request_token })` unconditionally — this is the click's only AD interaction (SR-4.3). No `get` refetch, no "already decided" branch.

The click resolves through the receiving persona (b.av2 SR-7.1): `ClickDeps.receivingPersonaKey` looked up with `getPersona`. The click arrives on the owning persona's connection through its `interactive` event, and the event router (`src/persona-event-router.ts`) passes that connection's persona key, so a click that reaches another persona's connection is refused below. Parsing comes first: an action_id that does not parse returns `false` before any persona is resolved. After the `cscb.click_handler.invoked` event:

- A receiving persona that cannot be resolved is logged and the click is bypassed (no decide, no update): `[slack] permission-click-handler: cannot resolve the receiving persona (receiving key=<key>, claude_instance_id=<id>, action_id=<action_id>, request_token=<token>) — logging and bypassing this click. This is a CSCB bug — investigate.`
- A click whose instance ID is not the receiving persona's `cscb_<key>` fails closed — neither decided nor updated, so the request stays open until agent-director times it out: `[slack] permission-click-handler: click for <id> received for "<name>" (key=<key>) (instance cscb_<key>) — not deciding (request_token=<token>)`.
- Otherwise `decideWithToken` runs inside `withOutageDetection` keyed by the persona key, so click-path outage state is per persona, and every `chat.update` below (verdict and relay-fallen-back repaint) goes through that persona's client (`ClickDeps.clientFor`) to the conversation and `ts` recorded on the live entry. A prompt posted in a DM is handled the same way, with no post and no `conversations.open` (see [In-memory state](#in-memory-state-sr-1)). When the client is unavailable, no update is made and `markHandled` is not called; one line is logged, `[slack] permission-click: no Slack client for "<name>" (key=<key>) — update for <id> (request_token=<token>, verdict=<tag>) skipped; the next poller tick reconciles the rendering`.

Decide outcomes:

- `ErrAlreadyDecided` → swallowed silently. The next tick's reconciliation (SR-2.4) owns the visible Slack state.
- `ErrRelayFallenBack` (b.qi1) → logged loudly, prompt message repainted, no session-level action. See the note below.
- `ErrInvalidFlags` / `ErrAmbiguousRequest` → logged once, no retry, no operator-visible signal.
- Unknown error types → logged, no retry.

On a successful decide *and* a live entry present, the handler renders one `chat.update` against just that row's `messageTs` (SR-4.5) carrying text byte-identical to the poller's SR-2.4 verdict surface — `*Permission* — Allowed` for allow, `*Permission* — Denied by operator` for deny — then calls `markHandled`. Matching the verdict text exactly means there is no visible flicker when the next tick's reconciliation lands on the same message. A stale click (no entry in `livePermissions`) still fires the decide call with the decoded token — AD is the source of truth and returns idempotent results — but produces no `chat.update` from the click path; the next tick's verdict rendering handles the message.

**b.qi1 `ErrRelayFallenBack` — loud surfacing, no session-level action.** agent-director 0.10.0 raises this from `decide` when the request's relay window has elapsed: Claude Code already gave up on the relay and re-asked at its own tmux pane, so AD refuses to record a verdict nothing will read. It is a *decide-path* error, not a spawn error — the spawn ladder in [Server-Managed Startup](#server-managed-startup-sr-11-event-1) cannot see it, and `handlePermissionClick` is its only reachable call site. The spawn itself is alive and healthy; only that one prompt can no longer be answered from Slack. The handler therefore logs a `[slack]`-prefixed line, emits `cscb.ad_decide.attempted{result_class:"ErrRelayFallenBack"}`, and — when a live entry is still present — renders one `chat.update` over that row's `messageTs` reading `*Permission* — relay window elapsed; answer this prompt at the session's tmux pane`, stripping the now-inert buttons, tagged `click_handler_relay_fallen_back`.

No delete, no respawn, no retry. Re-deciding fails identically (the window is already closed), and a respawn would destroy a healthy session over one un-relayed prompt while plausibly falling back again — the re-fallback loop the b.jgf delete+fresh policy would have walked into here. `markHandled` is deliberately **not** called: this is not a verdict and the AD request row stays open, so a later poller closure (timeout, `find_missing`, or the answer given at the pane) must remain free to render over the same message.

The Slack user id is intentionally NOT surfaced in the rendered text. An earlier draft of this design rendered "Allowed by <user>" / "Denied by <user>" as immediate operator feedback, but the SR-2.4 reconciler overwrites the message one tick later with the user-less verdict surface, producing visible flicker for the operator who clicked. The simpler, flicker-free design renders the verdict text on both the click path and the reconciliation path.

#### Wedge detector (b.fae F4)

A spawn can wedge permanently in `check_permission` with an **empty** `permission_requests` array: the underlying incident is a permission decision that AD recorded but could not deliver (the relay hook was killed before AD's poll deadline), leaving Claude Code blocked on a native TUI prompt with no open AD row and no hook that will ever fire again to advance state. To the poller this is a valid conforming response with zero rows, so before b.fae it was a silent no-op every tick — no log, no Slack message, no trail event ("the bot appears dead").

The detector counts consecutive ticks a spawn is observed in `check_permission` with an empty projection. The trip threshold is derived from the poll interval — `wedgeTripTicks(intervalMs) = max(5, ceil(90000 / intervalMs))`, roughly 90 s of wall-clock — because a genuine wedge takes minutes to develop while a normal decided-then-closing spawn passes through the empty state only briefly. The counter is reset **only on a positive observation** that the spawn is not wedged — open rows present, its state changed, or it's confirmed gone via `ErrSpawnNotFound`. A spawn skipped in a tick because of a transient `get` error or a non-conforming response does **not** have its counter reset, so a single flaky AD connection can't silently defer a genuine wedge alarm. A spawn whose persona is not up (b.av2 SR-6.4) is neither counted nor reset: its state is held, listed or not, until the persona is up.

On trip it logs `[slack] permission-poller: spawn <id> for "<name>" (key=<key>) wedged in check_permission with zero open rows for <n> ticks (~<s>s) — sending one-shot warning` and sends a **one-shot** warning to the persona's destination (b.av2 SR-7.2). The warning text names the persona: `Persona "<name>" (key=<key>): ⚠️ This persona appears blocked on a native Claude Code permission prompt …` (built with the notifier's `formatPersonaNotice`). It is one top-level message posted through the persona's client and the shared destination resolver to the persona's destination: its channel, or its DM with `dm.contact` (opened with `conversations.open` if needed, as for prompts). Each attempt that reaches Slack emits the `cscb.poller.wedge_detected` trail event. A persona whose client is unavailable counts as a failed post, with no Slack call and no trail event (`[slack] permission-poller: no Slack client for "<name>" (key=<key>) — stuck-prompt warning for <id> not posted; retrying on a later tick`); so does a `dm` destination the resolver refuses (DMs off or no contact), which the resolver logs on each attempt. Warning delivery is **fail-safe**: the `warningFired` latch is set **only after a successful `chat.postMessage`**. If the open or post fails, the trail event still records the attempt (`ok:false`) and the poller retries on later ticks — throttled to one attempt per `wedgeWarnRetryTicks(intervalMs)` (~30 s of wall-clock, `WEDGE_WARN_RETRY_WALL_CLOCK_MS`) so a Slack outage can't storm the API at the poll interval — until one succeeds, after which the latch sets and the episode posts nothing further. Each attempt also goes through the destination hold, asked after the throttle: while the persona is held the detector attempts nothing, logs nothing (not even the trip line) and leaves the throttle as it is, so the warning goes out at the first allowed tick once the persona's retry is due. A destination failure reaches `server.log` only as the hold's `persona-destination-failed` episode line; a failure of the message itself logs `[slack] permission-poller: wedge warning postMessage failed for "<name>" (key=<key>) (<id>)[ (reason=<reason>)]: <description>` (the tail is built by `describeSlackCallFailure`, below). The warning tells the operator to inspect the native prompt with `agent-director read-pane --claude-instance-id <id>` then kill and respawn the session; it explicitly does **not** offer `send-keys`, which AD's relay guard (`pkg/api/sendkeys.go`) hard-rejects while `relay_mode=on && state=check_permission`. The detector re-arms per spawn the moment it leaves the wedged condition (an open row appears, or the spawn disappears from the tick), so a later genuine wedge warns again. State is in-memory (`wedgeStates` map) and resets on daemon restart.

#### Destination hold (b.av2 SR-7.1 failures)

When Slack refuses a post to a persona's destination, the persona's permission prompts, stuck-prompt warnings and server notices are held and retried with backoff. The logic is `src/persona-destination-hold.ts`; `server.ts` builds one instance and hands it to both the poller and the notifier, so a persona's prompts and notices share one episode.

**What is held.** A destination failure is any failed `conversations.open` (including an open that returns no conversation ID, `no_conversation_id`) and any failed `chat.postMessage` except a payload error about the message itself (`MESSAGE_PAYLOAD_ERRORS`: `invalid_blocks`, `msg_too_long`, `no_text` and the like). So `missing_scope` on the open (an app not re-installed with `im:write`), `not_in_channel`, `channel_not_found`, `is_archived`, other permission errors and the Slack-unreachable class are all held. A payload error neither opens nor extends an episode: a prompt is logged and trailed per attempt and tried again next tick, and a notice is logged once and dropped. A refused `dm` destination (DMs off or no contact) makes no Slack call and is not a failure. The resolver does not cache a failed open, so each retry opens afresh.

**Per persona (b.av2 SR-3.3).** Each persona has its own entry: episode, retry schedule, FIFO of held notices, timer and in-flight guard. Nothing spans two personas, so one persona's episode never delays, holds or logs for another.

**Episodes and backoff.** An episode opens at a destination failure when none is open for the persona, and logs one `persona-destination-failed` line (see Logging, [Persona destination lines](#persona-destination-lines-bav2-sr-103)). The next retry is due after the persona's SR-3.2 wait (`createPersonaRetrySchedule`: 5 s doubling to 300 s, no cap, never shorter than a `retryAfter` the error carries). While the retry is not due, or while one retry is in flight, the persona is held and nothing is attempted for it. A failed retry advances the schedule and logs nothing (except at a newly named destination, below). The first successful attempt for the persona, prompt or notice, ends the episode with one cleared line and resets the schedule; a later failure opens a new episode and logs again. A failure from an attempt that started before the last episode change (an episode opened or cleared since; for example concurrent posts all waiting on one DM open, or a slow post that fails after a retry cleared the episode) is stale: it logs no line, opens no episode and does not advance the schedule, and its notice is re-queued in submission order (attempted again at once when no episode is open, so a genuine failure then opens one). A success from an attempt that started before the open episode opened does not end it.

**Destination changed during an episode.** An in-place change to `permission_prompts` can move the destination while an episode is open. A current (not stale) failure at a destination no line of the episode has named yet logs one more opening line naming it, with that failure's step and code, and becomes the episode's latest named cause. It never logs again for that destination in the episode, so a failure back at an earlier one logs nothing. The episode and its backoff carry on unreset. The cleared line names the destination the successful post went to, with the latest named cause.

**Attempts always finish.** An attempt that never settles would leave the persona in flight with no timer. `begin()`'s `post()` settles the attempt in a `finally`, even when the resolver throws; the poller builds a prompt's text and blocks before `begin()` and wraps everything after it in `try`/`finally` `release()`, so a throw before the post frees the persona.

**Prompts and the stuck-prompt warning** are never queued in the hold. The poller asks `begin(key)` before each attempt and makes none while the persona is held; the prompt stays untracked (no live entry) until an attempt posts it, and the next due tick derives it again (Per-tick flow step 4, **Held persona**). A request that stops being pending meanwhile is not derived again and is never posted. Closing updates and click updates are not gated: an update is not a post.

**Notices** go through `deliver`. A persona that is not held, has no notices waiting and no retry of held notices running gets the notice posted at once, in raised order. Otherwise the notice joins the persona's FIFO behind earlier ones. A failed first attempt of a notice also joins it. When the retry is due, the persona's timer attempts the held notices in order, one at a time: the first failure stops and reschedules, and after the first success the rest follow. A prompt that posts ends the episode too, and the held notices are then attempted at once. The FIFO holds at most `MAX_HELD_NOTICES_PER_PERSONA` (20): past it the oldest held notice (never the one being attempted) is dropped, logged and never posted. Each attempt re-reads the persona, its client and its destination, so a changed destination or contact is honoured. A retry that finds no client (the persona is not up) waits again with no line; one whose persona or client lookup throws waits the same backoff, with one line per run of throws; a persona no longer applied has its held notices dropped with one line. The timer exists only while notices are held and waiting. `cancel(key)` (a persona teardown) and `cancelAll()` (shutdown) drop held notices without posting them, with one line per persona that had notices held.

Lost-message notices (Data Flow, Inbound step 7) are notices like any other: raised through the notifier under the persona's identity, held while the persona has no validated client, sent to a channel or a `dm` destination (the DM opened when attempted) and held and retried here while the destination refuses. Inbound delivery doesn't special-case them either, and the loader doesn't warn about the configuration below: a posted notice reaches any persona with `delivery: all` in the destination channel. So two personas that each receive every message in the other's destination channel, with neither persona's instance able to take messages, lose each other's notices and keep raising notices about each other until either instance takes messages again. This is by design; the README (Permission prompts) and the `debug-slack-channel-bots` skill tell operators to give each persona a destination no other persona receives every message in.

**Two holds for notices.** The notifier's pre-validation queue (`persona-notifier.ts`) holds a notice while the persona has no validated client; the destination hold holds it while the destination refuses. They are separate queues, each capped at 20, and a notice can pass through both: flushed from the first when the persona comes up, then held by the second if the post fails. A persona teardown empties both, the notifier's with `forget(key)` and the hold's with `cancel(key)`, and forgets the persona's cached DM conversation (`personaDestinations.forget(key)`); nothing held is posted.

**Dry run** makes no Slack call, so nothing is ever held: the notifier logs its dry-run line before reaching the hold, and `clientFor` answers nothing in dry run, so the poller never asks the hold.

**Trail.** No trail event or field is added. Every real attempt still emits its `cscb.chat_post.attempted` or `cscb.poller.wedge_detected` event (with `error` from `safeFailureCode`); a held, skipped attempt emits nothing.

**Production caveat.** The long-lived Web API client (`persona-slack-clients.ts`) keeps the library's own retries (about 30 minutes) and rate-limit waits. So a network failure reaches the hold only after those retries are spent, and a rate-limited call is absorbed by the library; platform errors such as `missing_scope`, `not_in_channel` and `channel_not_found` reach it at once.

#### Action ID encoding (SR-3)

Block Kit buttons emit `action_id` strings of the shape `perm_(allow|deny)_<claude_instance_id>_<request_token>`, where `<request_token>` is a UUIDv4. The anchored decoder regex uses the UUIDv4 character class (`[0-9a-f]{8}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{4}-[0-9a-f]{12}`) as an outer-structure anchor — it disambiguates the rightmost boundary of the middle (underscore-bearing) `claude_instance_id` capture so embedded underscores in `cscb_<key>` remain safe. `<key>` is the persona key. CSCB does NOT validate the token's bytes; the regex shape only fixes the parse boundary (SR-1.3 + SR-3.1). The `request_id` field is no longer part of the action_id payload and is retained on `LivePermission` for logging only (SR-1.4).

#### Version pin (SR-6)

AD's library-side `Client.create()` reads `dist/version-floor.json` (`.min_binary_version`) and is the sole enforcement point for the runtime floor. The system-installed AD binary is probed by `resolveSystemBinary()`, compared against the declared minimum, and rejected via `ErrSystemInstallTooOld` (which the startup gate `instanceof`-matches → `ad-system-install-too-old` class label). CSCB's `package.json` caret on `agent-director` only governs npm resolution of AD's TypeScript shim; it does NOT participate in the runtime floor decision. The startup gate (`runStartupGate` in `src/agent-director-startup.ts`) is the sole compatibility boundary — no runtime code path degrades to the prior singular-projection or coalesced-prompt behavior on version mismatch (SR-6.2). The three new typed-error class labels are `ad-system-install-not-found`, `ad-system-install-too-old`, and `ad-system-install-unreachable`; see [Startup errors](../README.md#startup-errors).

Operators have an alternate diagnostic entry point via `bun run install-check` (`scripts/install-check.ts`). The script calls `runInstallCheck()` from `src/install-check.ts` — the same shared module the Epic-3 install-cscb skill consumes — and emits a single block on success or a class-labelled stderr block on failure. Beyond the three typed errors, the shared module surfaces `ad-version-floor-unreadable` when the AD package's `dist/version-floor.json` is missing, malformed, or lacks `.min_binary_version` (a packaging defect; remediation is to reinstall `agent-director` from npm, and the manual-skill-install block is intentionally NOT appended on this class).

#### API surface probe (SR-6.1)

A passing `Client.create()` proves only that the system-installed `agent-director` binary meets AD's declared `min_binary_version`. It does NOT prove the published npm package's TS shim is in sync with that binary — agent-director's `0.6.0` release shipped a stale shim whose `Client` dropped `getPermission`, whose `buildDecide()` dropped `--request-token`, and whose error catalog omitted three err_names CSCB branches on. Each defect fails silently at runtime (clicks resolve against the wrong row, error-envelope branches miss because the shim's `errorFromEnvelope` fallback constructs a base `AgentDirectorError` with `.errName` copied verbatim from the input rather than raising), so the gate runs three short-circuit probes after `Client.create()` resolves and before the same-user stat. The probes are dependency-injected via `StartupGateDeps` so tests can drive each failure path directly; the production defaults live alongside in `src/agent-director-startup.ts`.

| Probe | Default check | Failure `classLabel` |
|---|---|---|
| `probeGetPermission` | `typeof client.getPermission === 'function'` | `ad-shim-missing-get-permission` |
| `probeErrorCatalog` | Reads the resolved `agent-director` dist file and confirms each of `ErrInvalidFlags`, `ErrPermissionRequestNotFound`, `ErrAmbiguousRequest` appears as a word-boundary identifier in the bundled JS — same static-file approach as `probeDecideArgv` (a behavioral `errorFromEnvelope` round-trip cannot detect catalog drift because the shim's unknown-name fallback constructs a base `AgentDirectorError` with `.errName` copied verbatim from the input) | `ad-shim-catalog-incomplete` |
| `probeDecideArgv` | `import.meta.resolve('agent-director')` → `readFileSync` of the dist entry → string-contains `--request-token` (no subprocess, no state-DB write) | `ad-shim-decide-drops-token` |

On any probe failure, the gate closes the Client, records the operator-readable message to `startup-errors.log`, and exits non-zero — the same shape as the SR-6 version-pin failure. The remediation in every case is the same: reinstall a matching `agent-director` and confirm the resolved package actually carries the missing surface.

#### What this replaces

Three pre-Epic-4 mechanisms have been retired:

- The four-case per-spawn reconciliation matrix (and its sibling-stomping `updateNoLongerActive()` helper that edited a prior row's Slack message in response to another row's arrival) is gone. Sibling rows are now first-class state from the moment they appear in the plural projection.
- The click handler's prior get-then-stale-rerender branch (a refetch + "already decided" `chat.update` issued without calling decide) is gone. `decideWithToken` is the click's only AD interaction.
- The single-prompt coalescing of multiple concurrent `tool_use` blocks is gone. N parallel tool_uses → N Slack prompts.

AskUserQuestion is denied at the agent-director template (SR-3.1's `deny: ['AskUserQuestion']`) — the tool is unavailable to every CSCB-spawned bot and the prior `/ask` HTTP route + hook script have been removed.

The template also carries a narrow `allow` list (b.fae) pre-authorizing bot reads of their own persistent-memory directories. The rules are **derived per config** by `deriveMemoryReadAllowRules(personaConfig, home?)` rather than hardcoded. Each applied persona contributes its effective `claude_config_dir` (its own, else the top-level one, already resolved on the persona) — the directory `session-manager.ts` `buildSpawnParams` exports as `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` at spawn. A persona with none uses Claude Code's default `<home>/.claude` (`resolveClaudeConfigDir`, home read at call time), and a config with no personas still gets the default-dir rule. The set comes from `effectiveClaudeConfigDirs` (`persona-identity.ts`), the rule the reload plan's `configDirsChanged` shares: the distinct dirs are de-duplicated and sorted, and one rule is emitted per dir:

```
Read(//<abs-config-dir>/projects/*/memory/**)
```

So a deployment whose personas use `~/.claude-infhub` and `~/.claude` yields those two rules, but a single-config or default-only deployment yields just one. The boot install writes the rules for the applied personas, and a confirmed change that alters the set of effective config dirs rewrites them for the confirmed personas (apply step 5, [Template refresh](#template-refresh-bav2-sr-86-step-5)); a failed refresh leaves the previous rules until the set changes again or the next start. Memory-note reads always require confirmation in Claude Code; without this rule they round-trip to a human as a native TUI permission prompt (the prompt class that wedged the relay in b.fae). **The scope is deliberately limited to `projects/*/memory/**` and must not be widened to the config-dir root** — that root holds live credentials (`settings.json` `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `.claude.json` OAuth/token content), so a root-level allow would silently pre-authorize reads of billing credentials. The `//` prefix is Claude Code's absolute-path-from-filesystem-root anchor for Read rules.

### Scheduled Prompts (cscb_cron)

The server fires scheduled prompts from a crontable into personas once per minute. The flow crosses four modules — crontable → scheduler tick → dispatcher → the server's own `/interject` → cron log — and is driven entirely by `src/cron-scheduler.ts`'s single minute-aligned timer (decision 8; no per-job timers exist).

Managed sessions learn the crontable path via the `CSCB_CRONTABLE_PATH` export documented in the [Environment Variables](#environment-variables) section.

#### Wiring and startup

The scheduler is constructed and started in `main()` (`server.ts`) **after** `Bun.serve()` is listening, because it is its own `/interject` client — it must POST to a live listener. This is the opposite placement from `startPermissionPoller`, which starts before `Bun.serve`. It is wired on every start, dry run included: a start always has an applied configuration (the last-applied record or `config.json`), so there is no path that runs without it. The dispatcher is handed the server's ACTUAL bound port (`httpServer.port`, covering port-`0` configs), the config-resolved `cron_log_path` and `cron_table_path`, and a `resolveTarget` over the applied persona config (`resolvePersonaTarget(personaConfig, target)?.key`). Any construction or `start()` failure is non-fatal — the server keeps serving without a scheduler; `shutdown()` calls `cronScheduler.stop()` alongside `stopHealthCheck`/`stopPermissionPoller`.

`start()` runs, in order: (1) the crontable bootstrap — `ensureCrontableExists`, which this `start()` is the SOLE caller of; a missing crontable is created with the template header and an info line, and the scheduler proceeds with zero schedules; (2) the initial read + parse of the table through the same shared load path the tick's reload step uses, each parse error becoming one `parse-error` outcome line (identity/target `-`); (3) exactly one `scheduler started, N schedules loaded` info line — the PD-4 outage-window marker, since there is no catch-up, this line is the only bound on a missed-fire window; (4) arming the tick. `start()` is failure-isolated and never throws out to the server.

#### The tick (reload → match → dispatch)

The timer is a self-re-arming `setTimeout` chain aligned to the top of each minute (not `setInterval`), so ticks are serialized — a pass never overlaps the next, which is the property the hot-reload depends on: the in-memory table is swapped between whole passes, never mid-pass. Each pass, in decision-8 loop order: (1) re-load the crontable if it changed (see [Hot-reload](#hot-reload) below) — this step never dispatches and never re-arms the timer; (2) stamp the current wall-clock minute and skip the whole pass unless it is strictly after the last-ticked minute — the **monotonic guard**, so a backwards clock jump cannot re-fire already-fired minutes (matches Vixie cron), and, because it sits *after* the reload, also the protection against a double-fire across a reload — a minute already processed is never processed again, whichever generation of the table is in memory; (3) match every in-memory schedule against that minute in **server-local time** via croner (DST is croner's, D-Q3); (4) per match, consult the at-most-once `Map<schedule instance, epoch minute>` — keyed on the compiled-schedule instance (identity), NOT raw line text, so two identical operator lines are two distinct schedules that EACH fire (plain cron semantics, PD-6); only the SAME loaded schedule reappearing twice in one pass is skipped and logged as a loud `SCHEDULER BUG` info line, otherwise the instance is recorded and the match collected; (5) prune map entries older than the current minute. All matches for the minute are handed, in crontable line order, to the dispatcher.

**No catch-up (PD-4):** a tick fires only the minute observed at its start; if a pass overruns the next minute top, that minute is simply skipped — specified behavior, not a bug. A minute with no matches does nothing and logs nothing. The `(schedule instance, minute)` at-most-once assertion is a **logged bug signal**, not the dispatch mechanism — the minute-aligned single pass already makes a double-fire structurally impossible; because the key is the compiled-schedule instance rather than raw line text, two identical operator lines are distinct schedules that each fire (PD-6) and never trip the assertion — only the same loaded schedule firing twice in one pass does. The `SCHEDULER BUG` line is an info line kind, deliberately NOT an outcome class, so it never pollutes the `grep http-error` network-failure triage view. It is likewise not the cross-reload double-fire protection — that is the monotonic guard's job. There is NO rate cap, backlog cap, busy-detection, session-state check, or queue-depth throttle anywhere in the tick (owner gate answers 1-2).

#### Hot-reload

The crontable **file**, not the in-memory copy, is the runtime source of truth. Step (1) of each tick stats the crontable and compares `(mtimeMs, size)` for *inequality* against the last successful load — not ordering, so a backwards `touch` still reloads. Unchanged means no read, no log, no work. Changed means re-read, re-parse, re-compile, and swap the array; the swap only ever replaces the whole table, and it never dispatches and never touches the timer chain.

There is deliberately **no `fs.watch`, no watcher, and no dirty flag.** A swap can only take effect at a tick anyway, and a single-file watch drops the atomic-save rename that is the mandated write pattern, so a per-tick stat is both sufficient and cheaper to reason about. A successful tick reload writes one `crontable reloaded, N schedules` info line; `start()`'s initial load deliberately emits no such line (it has its own `scheduler started` marker).

Parse errors are logged **once per reload, not once per tick**, because the recorded stat is updated on every successful read — including a read whose content has bad lines — so an unchanged broken file stays quiet. A mid-write read needs no special handling: the parser is PD-6-tolerant, so healthy lines survive and keep firing, and the completed write is a further stat change the next tick picks up.

**Deleted file (D-Q1).** ENOENT drops to **zero schedules immediately** — nothing stale ever fires behind a deleted crontable — forgets the recorded stat, and emits a latched `crontable vanished … — 0 schedules until it is restored` warn-kind line. The warn kind is what distinguishes this from the first-install info line. The *next* pass attempts the re-create through `cron-bootstrap.ts`: an exclusive create, so if a human or a bot restored the file with content first, the bootstrap reports `already-exists` and that content loads intact; otherwise the empty template header is created (zero schedules) and logged with re-creation wording distinct from the first-install line. A persistent re-create failure stays silent — the vanish warn already latched the condition, and it must not repeat once a minute.

**Other stat/read failures.** Any non-ENOENT errno (EACCES, EIO, …) **keeps the currently loaded schedules firing** and emits one latched warn naming the cause and the schedule count. Because a failed load never updates the recorded stat, recovery (a `chmod`, say) is picked up by the next tick's inequality check, which clears the latch and logs the normal reload line.

#### The fire path (dispatcher → /interject → cron log)

For each match the dispatcher (`src/cron-dispatch.ts`) resolves the prompt path and reads the prompt FRESH at fire time (never cached — an edited prompt takes effect next fire), resolves the written targets to persona keys and deduplicates them, applies the D-Q5 pre-checks (skip, never truncate), and POSTs `{persona, message, sender}` (`persona` = the resolved key, or the target as written when it names no persona) to `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/interject`. `sender` is the schedule identity `cscb-cron:<prompt-file-basename-without-extension>` (D-Q4) — without it `/interject` defaults `senderLabel` to `interject` and a cron fire is indistinguishable from peer-bot traffic. HTTP status maps to outcome class: 200 → `delivered`, 503 → `no-session` (decision 1 — NO retry, no queue, no `scheduleRestart`; this includes a persona that is not up, which `/interject` answers 503 even while its instance runs, b.av2 SR-6.4), 404 → `unknown-persona`, any other status or a network failure → `http-error`. Every outcome plus one per-fire summary (`delivered`/`failed` counts) is appended to the cron log. A line with no persona list (all-bots) is matched but logged `fanout-deferred` and NOT dispatched — no read, no POST — with a `delivered=0 failed=0` summary; fan-out is not yet enabled.

**Same-minute matches deliver independently.** Each matched schedule is delivered on its own — its own freshly-read prompt, its own `/interject` POST with `sender` = its schedule identity, and its own outcome line and per-fire summary. When several crontable lines match the same minute for the same persona, each one arrives as its own message; they are not combined.

The cron-log outcome classes are defined once in [cron.log](#cronlog-cron_log_path) — the taxonomy there is authoritative; this subsection does not restate the class names.

**Bind constraint.** The scheduler POSTs to `127.0.0.1`, so a `bind` on a single non-loopback interface leaves no loopback listener and every fire fails connection-refused → `http-error`. `bind` must include loopback (the default `127.0.0.1`, or `0.0.0.0`) for scheduled fires to work. This is log-visible and accepted; no loopback detection is added.

## Session Lifecycle

### Connection

1. Claude Code sends POST to `/mcp` (no session ID) → `initPendingSession()` creates a pending session
2. MCP handshake completes → `server.oninitialized` fires → `handleInitialized()` waits for the session's SSE stream, then calls `roots/list`. An SSE stream that never opens, a failed `roots/list` and an empty roots list each disconnect the pending session through `closePendingSession` (`registry.ts`): the pending entry is removed, its SSE keep-alive stopped and its transport closed.
3. The first root's directory (tilde-expanded, made absolute) is matched by real path against the applied personas' working directories, and admitted only while that persona is up (`decideSessionAdmission` in `registry.ts`, over `matchPersonaByRootsPath` and the server's shared up predicate; b.av2 SR-6.3, SR-6.4). On a match to exactly one persona that is up, the pending session is promoted in place to the registered session for that persona key, with the real path as its `cwd`; a newer session for the same persona replaces the older one (logged as `Session replaced existing connection for persona …`). Registration ends the persona's not-connected episode (`forgetNotConnectedEpisode`, b.f2b), so a later disconnection is reported again. With no matching persona or an ambiguous match, the pending session is disconnected through `closePendingSession`, as in step 2.
   - **Persona not up (b.av2 SR-6.4).** When the matched persona is `broken` or `retrying`, or its Slack connection is not serving, the session is refused the same way (`closePendingSession`: removed from the pending map, its keep-alive stopped and its transport closed); it is never promoted or mapped, and a session already registered for that persona is left as it is. The instance sees its MCP connection close; nothing is done to the instance or its agent-director row. One line is logged per refused attempt, so each new connection attempt the instance makes while the persona is down logs again (the line is not rate-limited), with no token and no diagnostic class label: `[slack] Session refused: persona "<name>" (key=<key>) is not up (<reason>) — not registered; its instance is kept and may register once the persona is up`, where `<reason>` is `describePersonaNotUp`'s `<outcome>: <first cause>`, or `its bring-up has not run` / `its Slack connection is not serving`. A missing working directory still matches its own persona through `resolveRealPath`'s lexical fallback, so its instance is refused here rather than logged as unmatched. The instance registers once the persona is up: the launch that follows its bring-up (the start pass, or the launch after a retry) goes through the collision ladder, whose `waiting` and `working` branches send `/mcp reconnect` to the running instance (the `working` branch once the row reads `waiting` or its pane shows it stale, b.f2b), and whose `ended`/`missing` branches resume the row (Server-Managed Startup step 5). An instance the ladder leaves alone (`pending`, `check_permission`, `ask_user`, or a `working` wait that gave up with the session alive) is picked up by the health check's alive-but-not-connected path, which reconnects it through restart; an `ask_user` or `check_permission` row is never typed into, only once it moves on (Auto-Restart step 3). With `session_restart_delay` 0 nothing reconnects it, and the not-connected notice reports it (Health-Check Poller).
   - **Every server start.** Admission reads the same predicate at start, so an instance that survived the previous server process is refused until its persona's bring-up finishes, healthy personas included: `its bring-up has not run` before the controller has started that persona, `its Slack connection is not serving` while its bring-up is in progress. Once the persona is `up`, the instance registers through the same two routes: the start pass's launch reaches its row through the ladder, which reconnects a `waiting` or `working` row (a `working` one in the background, b.f2b: the pass parks that launch), and a `pending` row is reconnected by the health check, a `check_permission` or `ask_user` row only once its prompt is answered.
4. Session receives the messages its persona's pipeline (`persona-routing.ts`) delivers from the persona's configured channels and, with `dm.enabled` on, its DMs
5. A keep-alive timer is started (`startSseKeepAlive`) that writes SSE comment frames (`:ping\n\n`) every ~30 s to prevent idle connection drops from proxies or load balancers

### Server-Managed Startup (SR-11 Event 1)

Called from `main()` in `server.ts`. Before step 1, `main()` creates the state and inbox directories (nothing is created at import) and installs E2's `unhandledRejection` handler (`createUnhandledRejectionHandler`, once, before any persona connects), so one persona's stray rejection is logged token-safely as `[slack] unhandled rejection (process keeps running): …` and never takes the process down.

In `main()` the steps run in this order: 1, 2, 2a, 3, then the message-archive setup, outage state and notifier installation, the connection manager, step 7, `Bun.serve` with the PID file and the cron scheduler, `initRestart`, then 4, 4a-bis, 4a-quater, 4a-ter, 5, 8 and 9. Step 6 is not a point in the sequence: it happens whenever a persona comes up. The steps:

1. **SR-5.1 startup gate** — `runAgentDirectorStartupGate()` awaits `createClient(opts)` (production injects `Client.create`), which runs AD's library-side discovery + floor enforcement against `dist/version-floor.json`. The opts carry `logger: makeFilteredAdLogger(console)` (b.brv) so the Client's per-poll `SubprocessClient: <verb> ok` success dumps are dropped from `server.log` unless `CSCB_AD_VERBOSE` is set — see [Logging](#logging). On success the gate installs the constructed Client into the singleton via `setClient(client)` so subsequent `getClient()` call sites resolve to it, and the success-arm `adVersion` is read from `client.binaryVersion`. The construct-step catch ladder `instanceof`-matches `ErrBunVersionTooOld` (→ `ad-bun-version-too-old`), `ErrSystemInstallNotFound` (→ `ad-system-install-not-found`), `ErrSystemInstallTooOld` (→ `ad-system-install-too-old`, message names detected + required versions), and `ErrSystemInstallUnreachable` (→ `ad-system-install-unreachable`, message surfaces AD's `err.reason` verbatim); the three `ad-system-install-*` branches append the `install-skill-pointer.ts` manual-skill-install instructions block to their `recordStartupError` output. CSCB no longer performs a post-Client floor check — AD owns it. Then runs the SR-6.1 API surface probes (`getPermission` presence, error-catalog round-trip, `--request-token` in the dist) and stats `~/.agent-director/state.db` against `geteuid()`. Failure writes to `startup-errors.log` and exits non-zero.
2. **PID conflict check** — existing `checkPidConflict(PID_FILE)` invariant. CSCB enforces one instance per host.
2a. **Start resolution: the last-applied record (b.av2 SR-1.7, SR-8.7)** — `main()` builds the reload controller (`createReloadController`, `src/reload.ts`) over `reloadFilePaths(CONFIG_PATH)` and calls `resolveStart()`. Saving `config.json` does not change what runs: every start runs the last-applied record `config.json.last-applied` (see Configuration, Reload files) when it exists. Each file is read once, and the bytes validated are the bytes recorded or applied. The rules:

    - **Record present.** The record is validated in record mode (`parsePersonaConfigBytes` with `record: true`) and becomes the applied config; `config.json` is not read to decide what runs, so an edit that was never applied stays unapplied across restarts and reboots. Logs `[slack] Starting from the last-applied record "<record>"`. Credentials files are not read here: the bring-up (step 5) reads them as they stand, so a credentials change made while the server was down takes effect at this start.
    - **No record, valid `config.json`.** The file is validated in default mode, its exact bytes are written to the record with the durable write (`durableWriteFileSync`), and then it is applied. Logs `[slack] No last-applied record: recorded the configuration file "<config>" as "<record>"`. This is the path of a fresh install and of the first start after upgrading.
    - **No record, `config.json` missing or invalid.** Logs `[slack] Fatal: configuration error — <message>` (see Configuration, config.json, for the messages; a pre-persona file gets the SR-1.7 conversion error) and exits 1.
    - **No record, record unwritable.** Logs `[slack] reload-record-write-failed: cannot write the last-applied record "<record>" (<errno code>); the server does not start and nothing is applied` (the errno code only when the error carries one) and exits 1. When the rename succeeded but the directory sync failed (`DurableWriteUnsyncedError`), the line is instead `[slack] reload-record-write-failed: the last-applied record "<record>" was written but its directory could not be synced (<errno code>), so it may not survive a crash, and the next start will run it; the server does not start`, and the server exits 1 the same way.
